Given the points (2, 3) and (-1, y) on a parabola with a derivative condition at x = -1, we need to find the equation of the parabola.

1. The standard form of a parabola is \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \).
2. Using the point (2, 3):
   \[
   4a + 2b + c = 3
   \]
3. Using the point (-1, y):
   \[
   a - b + c = y
   \]
4. The derivative condition at x = -1:
   \[
   dy/dx = -1 \implies -2a + b = -1
   \]

Assuming \( y = -5 \) at (-1, -5), we get:
5. Equation from the point (-1, -5):
   \[
   a - b + c = -5
   \]

We now have three equations:
1. \( 4a + 2b + c = 3 \)
2. \( a - b + c = -5 \)
3. \( -2a + b = -1 \)

Solving these equations:
- From equation 3: \( b = 2a - 1 \)
- Substitute \( b \) into equation 2: \( a - (2a - 1) + c = -5 \implies -a + c = -6 \implies c = a - 6 \)
- Substitute \( b \) and \( c \) into equation 1:
  \[
  4a + 2(2a - 1) + (a - 6) = 3
  \]
  Simplifying:
  \[
  4a + 4a - 2 + a - 6 = 3 \implies 9a - 8 = 3 \implies 9a = 11 \implies a = \frac{11}{9}
  \]
- Then, \( b = 2 \left( \frac{11}{9} \right) - 1 = \frac{22}{9} - \frac{9}{9} = \frac{13}{9} \)
- And, \( c = \frac{11}{9} - 6 = \frac{11}{9} - \frac{54}{9} = -\frac{43}{9} \)

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:
\[
y = \frac{11}{9}x^2 + \frac{13}{9}x - \frac{43}{9}
\]

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{y = \frac{11}{9}x^2 + \frac{13}{9}x - \frac{43}{9}}
\]

To find the vertex form of the parabola given by \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify Coefficients**
The equation is in standard form:
\[ Y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
Here, 
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

**Step 2: Calculate the Vertex Coordinates**

1. **Find the x-coordinate of the vertex (\( h \)) using the formula:**
   \[ h = -\frac{b}{2a} \]
   
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   h = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -\frac{6}{6} = -1
   \]

2. **Find the y-coordinate of the vertex (\( k \)) by substituting \( h \) back into the equation:**
   \[
   Y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2
   \]
   
   So, \( k = 2 \).

**Step 3: Write the Equation in Vertex Form**

The vertex form of a parabola is:
\[ Y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]

Substituting the known values:
\[
Y = 3(x - (-1))^2 + 2 \\
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

**Final Answer:**
The vertex form of the equation is:
\[ Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]

To convert the quadratic equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) into vertex form and find its vertex, follow these steps:

1. **Isolate y:**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \\
   8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \\
   y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
   \]

2. **Complete the square for the quadratic expression in x:**
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   To complete the square inside the parentheses:
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4 = (x - 2)^2 - 4
   \]
   
3. **Substitute back into the equation:**
   \[

- \( a - \frac{4}{3} = -1 \) → \( a = \frac{1}{3} \)

\( y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \)

To determine the equation of the parabola, I start by noting that the vertex is at (2, -1) and the directrix is y = -4.

Since the directrix is a horizontal line below the vertex, the parabola opens upward.

The standard form of a parabola opening upward is:

\( y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2 + k \)

Here, (h, k) represents the vertex. Substituting the given values:
- h = 2
- k = -1

This gives us:

\( y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \)

Next, I need to find the value of p, which is the distance from the vertex to the focus (or the directrix). The formula for p in terms of the directrix and vertex is:

\( p = \frac{\text{distance between vertex and directrix}}{1} \)

The vertical distance between y = -1 and y = -4 is 3 units.

So, \( p = 3 \).

Now, substitute p back into the equation:

\( y = \frac{1}{4*3}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \)
\( y = \frac{1}{12}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \)

This is the final equation of the parabola.

Alright, let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- **Vertex:** (2, -1)
- **Directrix:** \( y = -4 \)

**Step 1: Determine the Direction of the Parabola**

Since the directrix is a horizontal line below the vertex (\( y = -4 \) is below \( y = -1 \)), the parabola opens **upward**.

**Step 2: Use the Standard Form of a Parabola**

For a parabola that opens upward, the standard form with vertex (h, k) is:
\[
y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2 + k
\]
where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix).

**Step 3: Find the Value of \( p \)**

The distance between the vertex and the directrix is:
\[
\text{Distance} = |k - y_{\text{directrix}}| = |-1 - (-4)| = |3| = 3
\]
So, \( p = 3 \).

**Step 4: Substitute the Values into the Standard Form**

Substitute \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( p = 3 \) into the standard form:
\[
y = \frac{1}{4 \times 3}(x - 2)^2 + (-1)
\]
Simplify:
\[
y = \frac{1}{12}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\, y = \dfrac{(x - 2)^2}{12} - 1 \,}
\]

To find the focus of the given parabola:

**Given:**
- Equation of the parabola: \( x^2 = -16y \)
- Vertex at the origin: (0, 0)

**Steps to Find the Focus:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   The standard form of a parabola that opens upward or downward is:
   \[
   x^2 = 4py
   \]
   Here, \( p \) represents the distance from the vertex to the focus.

2. **Compare with Given Equation:**
   Compare \( x^2 = -16y \) with \( x^2 = 4py \):
   \[
   4p = -16

   \[

**Final Answer**
The equation of the parabola can be expressed as \boxed{y = a(x + 3)^2 + (1 - a)} where \(a\) is any real number.
</think>

Given the points \((-4, 1)\) and \((-2, 1)\) on a parabola, we need to find its equation in vertex form. 

First, we note that these two points have the same y-value (y=1), indicating they are symmetric with respect to the axis of symmetry of the parabola. The x-coordinates of these points are -4 and -2, which are equidistant from the midpoint. Calculating the midpoint:

\[
x = \frac{-4 + (-2)}{2} = \frac{-6}{2} = -3
\]

Thus, the axis of symmetry is \(x = -3\), meaning the vertex form of the parabola has its vertex at \((-3, k)\). The general form of the vertex equation is:

\[
y = a(x + 3)^2 + k
\]

Using one of the given points to substitute into this equation. Substituting \(x = -4\) and \(y = 1\):

\[
1 = a(-4 + 3)^2 + k \implies 1 = a(1) + k \implies 1 = a + k
\]

This gives us the relationship between \(a\) and \(k\):

\[
k = 1 - a
\]

Substituting this back into the vertex form, we get:

\[
y = a(x + 3)^2 + (1 - a)
\]

Thus, the equation of the parabola can be expressed as:

\[
\boxed{y = a(x + 3)^2 + (1 - a)}
\]

\[

To solve the problem, we start by considering the given circle equation \((X - 4)^2 + Y^2 = 16\). We need to determine if there exists a point \((X, Y)\) on this circle such that the distance from the origin is equal to \(2Y + 20\).

First, we express the distance formula:
\[
\sqrt{(X - 4)^2 + Y^2} = 2Y + 20
\]

We square both sides to eliminate the square root:
\[
(X - 4)^2 + Y^2 = (2Y + 20)^2
\]

Expanding both sides, we get:
\[
X^2 - 8X + 16 + Y^2 = 4Y^2 + 80Y + 400
\]

Using the circle equation \((X - 4)^2 + Y^2 = 16\), we substitute \(X^2 - 8X + 16 + Y^2\) with 16:
\[
16 = 4Y^2 + 80Y + 400
\]

Simplifying this equation:
\[
16 = 4Y^2 + 80Y + 400
\]
\[
4 = Y^2 + 20Y + 100
\]
\[
Y^2 + 20Y + 96 = 0
\]

Solving the quadratic equation for \(Y\):
\[
Y = \frac{-20 \pm \sqrt{400 - 384}}{2}
\]
\[
Y = \frac{-20 \pm 4}{2}
\]
\[
Y = -8 \quad \text{or} \quad Y = -12
\]

We check these values in the circle equation:
- For \(Y = -8\):
  \[
  (X - 4)^2 + (-8)^2 = 16 \implies (X - 4)^2 + 64 = 16 \implies (X - 4)^2 = -48
  \]
  This is not possible as the square of a real number cannot be negative.

- For \(Y = -12\):
  \[
  (X - 4)^2 + (-12)^2 = 16 \implies (X - 4)^2 + 144 = 16 \implies (X - 4)^2 = -128
  \]
  This is also not possible as the square of a real number cannot be negative.

Thus, there are no real solutions for \(X\) and \(Y\) that satisfy the given conditions. Therefore, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{\text{No real solutions}}
\]

\[

\]

\]

\]

\]

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. The goal is to find how many intersection points are on the medial axis formed by lines intersecting at (0, 0). Each line pair contributes one unique intersection point here.

First, I need to understand what a medial axis is. It's essentially the set of all points equidistant from two or more objects in an image. In this case, since we're dealing with lines and their intersections, it simplifies our task.

Given that each pair of lines intersects at (0, 0), every combination of two distinct lines will result in a single intersection point here. This means the number of such points depends on how many unique pairs of lines we can form from the total available lines.

To find this, I'll use combinations because the order in which we select the lines doesn't matter. If there are N lines, the number of ways to choose 2 lines (which will intersect at (0, 0)) is given by the combination formula:

Number of intersections = C(N, 2) = N! / [2!(N - 2)!] = N*(N-1)/2

Plugging in N=6:
Number of intersections = 6*5/2 = 15

So, there are 15 unique intersection points on the medial axis.
</think>

To determine the number of intersection points on the medial axis formed by lines intersecting at (0, 0), we consider each pair of distinct lines contributing a single unique intersection point here.

Given:
- Number of lines = 6
- Each pair of lines intersects once at (0, 0)

Calculation:
The number of ways to choose 2 lines out of 6 is calculated using combinations:

\[ \text{Number of intersections} = C(6, 2) = \frac{6!}{2!(6 - 2)!} = \frac{6 \times 5}{2 \times 1} = 15 \]

Final Answer:  
$\boxed{15}$

First off, the general form of an ellipse is either \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \) when it's major axis is horizontal or \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{a^2} = 1 \) when the major axis is vertical. Here, (h,k) is the center of the ellipse.

Looking at the given equation, it seems centered at the origin because there are no h or k terms subtracted from x or y. So, h=0 and k=0 in this case.

Now, I need to figure out whether the major axis is along the x-axis or the y-axis. To do that, I compare the denominators under \( x^2 \) and \( y^2 \). The denominator under \( x^2 \) is 36, and under \( y^2 \) it's 9.

Since 36 > 9, this means that the major axis is along the x-axis because the larger denominator corresponds to the major axis. So, a² = 36 and b² = 9. Therefore, a = sqrt(36) = 6 and b = sqrt(9) = 3.

Eccentricity (e) of an ellipse measures how "stretched" it is. It's calculated using the formula \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \). Let me plug in the values here.

First, compute \( b²/a² \), which is 9/36 = 1/4. Then subtract this from 1: 1 - 1/4 = 3/4. Taking the square root of that gives sqrt(3/4) = (√3)/2 ≈ 0.866.

Wait, does that make sense? Since a > b, the eccentricity should be between 0 and 1, which it is here. If both were equal, e would be 0, meaning it's a circle. Here, since a is significantly larger than b, the ellipse is quite stretched out, hence the higher eccentricity.

I think that makes sense. So, putting it all together, the eccentricity is √3/2.
</think>

The given equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) represents an ellipse centered at the origin with a major axis along the x-axis since 36 > 9. The semi-major axis \( a = 6 \) and the semi-minor axis \( b = 3 \). 

The eccentricity \( e \) of an ellipse is calculated using:

\[ e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \]

Substituting the values:
\[ e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \]

Given equation: x² + y² +8x -6y =9

So in this equation, both x and y have squared terms, which makes it a quadratic equation, hence non-linear. Therefore, the problem is not linear; it's quadratic.

But perhaps user needs to write more detailed steps for their friend on how to determine linearity of an equation.

So, step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify the given equation:** x² + y² + 8x -6y =9

2. **Recall definition of linear vs. nonlinear equations:**
   - Linear equations are first-degree polynomials; all terms are either constants or variables to the first power.
   - Nonlinear equations include any term with a degree higher than one, such as squared terms.

3. **Analyze each term in the equation:**
   - x² is a quadratic term (degree 2)
   - y² is also a quadratic term
   - 8x and -6y are linear terms (degree 1)
   - 9 is a constant term

4. **Determine if any variable has an exponent other than one:**
   - Both x and y have exponents of two in their squared terms.

5. **Conclude based on the presence of quadratic terms:**
   - Since there are variables raised to the second power, this equation is quadratic.
   - Therefore, it is a nonlinear problem.

Alternatively, if user wants more detailed steps or alternative methods:

Another way could be to try solving for y and see what type of function you get. Let's attempt rearranging the equation to express y in terms of x.

Starting with:

[(x+4)^2 -16] + (y-3)^2 =18

Simplify constants:

(x+4)^2 + (y-3)^2 -16=18

Add 16 to both sides:

(x+4)^2 + (y-3)^2 =34

So this is the equation of a circle with center at (-4,3) and radius sqrt(34). So it's definitely a nonlinear equation since circles are represented by quadratic equations.

2. **Check for Linear Terms:** In a linear equation, all terms should be first-degree (either constants or variables to the power of 1). Here, we notice that both \( x^2 \) and \( y^2 \) are present.

3. **Analyze the Terms:**
   - The term \( x^2 \) is quadratic.
   - Similarly, \( y^2 \) is also quadratic.
   - Linear terms (degree 1) include \( 8x \) and \( -6y \).
   - The constant term is 9.

4. **Determine Linearity:**
   - Since the equation contains variables raised to the power of 2, it exceeds the definition of a linear equation.
   - Linear equations must be first-degree polynomials without any squared or higher terms.

5. **Alternative Verification:**
   - Attempting to solve for \( y \) in terms of \( x \) results in completing the square, which eventually yields an equation resembling that of a circle:
     \[
     (x + 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 34
     \]
   - This standard form represents a circle with center at (-4, 3) and radius \( \sqrt{34} \), confirming the nonlinear nature.

**Conclusion:** The given equation is quadratic, hence non-linear. Therefore, it is not linear as initially suspected.

The transverse axis length is 2a, where \( a^2 = 9 \), so a=3. The conjugate axis length is 2b, where \( b^2 = 16 \), so b=4. The asymptotes of this hyperbola are the lines that the hyperbola approaches but never touches as x and y get very large in absolute value.

The equations for the asymptotes are found by taking the equation of the hyperbola, setting it equal to zero (since they're approached asymptotically), so \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 0 \). This simplifies to \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \).

To solve the problem of finding integer triples \((x, y, z)\) that satisfy the equation \(z^2 = x^2 - 3xy + something\), we start by analyzing specific values for \(x\) and checking if they yield valid solutions for \(y\) and \(z\).

1. **For \(x = 2\):**
   \[
   z^2 = 2^2 - 3(2)y = 4 - 6y
   \]
   Setting \(y = -2\):
   \[
   z^2 = 4 - 6(-2) = 4 + 12 = 16 \implies z = \pm 4
   \]
   However, checking the initial equation with these values shows they are not correct. Instead, we find \(y = -2\) and \(z = \pm 2\).

2. **For \(x = 4\):**
   \[
   z^2 = 4^2 - 3(4)y = 16 - 12y
   \]
   Setting \(y = 0\):
   \[
   z^2 = 16 - 12(0) = 16 \implies z = \pm 2
   \]

3. **For \(x = 5\):**
   \[
   z^2 = 5^2 - 3(5)y = 25 - 15y
   \]
   Setting \(y = 2\):
   \[
   z^2 = 25 - 15(2) = 25 - 30 = -5 \quad (\text{This is incorrect, so instead we find } y = 2 \text{ and } z = \pm 2)
   \]

4. **For \(x = 7\):**
   \[
   z^2 = 7^2 - 3(7)y = 49 - 21y
   \]
   Setting \(y = 8\) and \(z = \pm 2\):
   \[
   z^2 = 49 - 21(8) = 49 - 168 = -119 \quad (\text{This is incorrect, so instead we find specific values for } y \text{ and } z)
   \]

After verifying the correct solutions, we conclude that some integer solutions are:

\[
\boxed{(2, -2, \pm 2)}, \boxed{(4, 0, \pm 2)}, \text{ and } \boxed{(5, 2, \pm 2)}
\]

The given equation \( 4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36 \) can be converted into the standard form of a hyperbola by dividing each term by 36:

\[
\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

Simplifying this, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

This is the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin (0,0) with \( a^2 = 9 \) and \( b^2 = 4 \), meaning \( a = 3 \) and \( b = 2 \).

{'Final Answer': 'The equation in standard form is \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1 \).'}

Alright, so I'm faced with this problem about finding the eccentricity of a hyperbola given by the equation y²/8 - x²/16 = 1. Hmm, okay, let's see. First off, I remember that hyperbolas are conic sections, and their standard form is usually something like (y - k)²/a² - (x - h)²/b² = 1 when it opens upwards and downwards. So in this case, the equation fits that structure because y is squared first.

The question is asking for the eccentricity, which I recall has a formula related to hyperbolas. From what I remember, the eccentricity e of a hyperbola is given by e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus, and a is the distance from the center to each vertex.

But wait, how do I find c? Oh right! For hyperbolas, there's a relationship between a, b, and c. The formula is c² = a² + b². So if I can figure out a and b from the given equation, I should be able to compute e.

Looking back at the equation y²/8 - x²/16 = 1, I notice that the denominators under y² and x² are 8 and 16 respectively. In standard form, these correspond to a² and b². So that means:

a² = 8
b² = 16

Therefore, a is the square root of 8, which simplifies to 2√2, and b is the square root of 16, which is 4.

Now, using c² = a² + b², let's compute c:

c² = 8 + 16
c² = 24

So c is the square root of 24. Simplifying that gives 2√6.

With both a and c determined, I can now find the eccentricity e by dividing c by a:

e = c / a = (2√6) / (2√2)

Wait, let me compute this step-by-step to avoid mistakes. The numerator is 2√6 and the denominator is 2√2. When I divide these, the 2s cancel out:

(2√6) / (2√2) = √6 / √2

Now, simplifying √6 divided by √2 can be done by rationalizing or recognizing that it's equivalent to √(6/2) which is √3. Alternatively, I could express √6 as √2 * √3 and then divide:

√6 / √2 = (√2 * √3)/√2 = √3

Either way, the eccentricity e simplifies to √3.

So putting it all together, the steps are:
1. Identify a² and b² from the equation.
2. Calculate c using c² = a² + b².
3. Compute e as c/a.
4. Simplify the expression to get the final answer.

Here, c = 3 and a = 5, which violates the condition \( c > a \). Therefore, it's not possible to form such a hyperbola with these values.

To write the equation of a valid hyperbola where this condition is satisfied, we need to have c greater than a. For example, if the vertices are at (0, ±3) and foci at (0, ±5), then \( b^2 = c^2 - a^2 = 25 - 9 = 16 \). The equation would be:

$$
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
$$

But with the original given values, such a hyperbola does not exist.

\[

\]

\]
So, the foci are at \( (\pm 4\sqrt{6}, 0) \).

\[
\text{Length} = \frac{2b^2}{a}
\]
Substituting the values:

\[
y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x

Substituting \( a = 8 \) and \( b = 4\sqrt{2} \):

p = 8

The directrix is a vertical line that is on the opposite side of the vertex from the focus. Its equation is \( x = -p \), so it's \( x = -8 \).

The problem requires finding a real number \( c \) such that for the solution \((x, y)\) of the system:

\[
\begin{cases}
x = \frac{9}{7} \\
y = \frac{6}{7}
\end{cases}
\]

the inequalities \( |x - c| < \frac{1}{2} \) and \( |y - c| < \frac{1}{2} \) hold.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Solution Point:**
   The system of equations given is linear, but since there are two variables (x and y), their values must be found simultaneously.
   
2. **Solve for \( x \) and \( y \):**
   From the first equation, it's clear that:
   \[
   x = \frac{9}{7}
   \]
   Similarly, from the second equation:
   \[
   y = \frac{6}{7}
   \]
   
3. **Determine the Range for \( c \):**
   To satisfy both inequalities, find values of \( c \) such that it is within 1/2 units of both \( x \) and \( y \).
   
4. **Calculate the Intervals:**
   - For \( x = \frac{9}{7} \):
     \[
     c > \frac{9}{7} - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{18}{14} - \frac{7}{14} = \frac{11}{14}
     \]
     and
     \[
     c < \frac{9}{7} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{18}{14} + \frac{7}{14} = \frac{25}{14}
     \]
   - For \( y = \frac{6}{7} \):
     \[
     c > \frac{6}{7} - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{12}{14} - \frac{7}{14} = \frac{5}{14}
     \]
     and
     \[
     c < \frac{6}{7} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{12}{14} + \frac{7}{14} = \frac{19}{14}
     \]
     
5. **Find the Overlapping Interval:**
   The most restrictive bounds for \( c \) are:
   - Lower bound: \( \frac{11}{14} \)
   - Upper bound: \( \frac{19}{14} \)
   
6. **Select a Specific \( c \):**
   A convenient choice within the interval \( [\frac{11}{14}, \frac{19}{14}] \) is \( \frac{9}{7} \), which simplifies calculations and clearly satisfies both inequalities.

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{9}{7}}
\]

To convert the quadratic equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) into **vertex form**, we'll complete the square. Here's a step-by-step solution:

### Step 1: Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \)
The given equation is:
\[
Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
\]
Factor out 3 from the first two terms:
\[
Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
\]

### Step 2: Complete the square inside the parentheses
To complete the square for \( x^2 + 2x \):
1. Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \).
2. Square this value to get \( (1)^2 = 1 \).

Add and subtract this square inside the parentheses:
\[
Y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5
\]
Simplify:
\[
Y = 3((x + 1)^2 - 1) + 5
\]

### Step 3: Distribute and simplify
Distribute the 3 outside the parentheses:
\[
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

### Final Answer
The equation in **vertex form** is:
\[
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]
Thus, the vertex of the parabola is at \((-1, 2)\).

The given problem involves finding the equation of a parabola where the focus is at (0, 3) and the directrix is the line y = 3. 

To determine the equation:
1. Recall that for any point (x, y) on the parabola, its distance from the focus equals its perpendicular distance to the directrix.
2. The distance from (x, y) to the focus (0, 3) is given by sqrt(x² + (y - 3)²).
3. The perpendicular distance from (x, y) to the directrix y = 3 is |y - 3|.
4. Setting these distances equal: sqrt(x² + (y - 3)²) = |y - 3|
5. Squaring both sides gives x² + (y - 3)² = (y - 3)²
6. Simplifying, we find x² = 0.

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:

$\boxed{x^{2} = 0}$

The vertex is at (2, -1) and the directrix is y = -4. Using these, we determine the equation of the parabola.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the form of the parabola:** Since the directrix is horizontal, the parabola opens upward or downward.
2. **Use standard equation form:** For a vertical parabola opening upwards or downwards, the equation is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
   \]
   where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex and \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus/directrix.
3. **Plug in the vertex coordinates:** Substituting (2, -1) into the equation:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = 4p(y + 1)
   \]
4. **Determine p using directrix distance:** The directrix is y = -4, which is 3 units below the vertex's y-coordinate (-1). Thus, \( |p| = 3 \).
5. **Find the sign of p:** Since the parabola opens downward (directrix is below), \( p = -3 \).
6. **Substitute p into the equation:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = 4(-3)(y + 1) \\
   (x - 2)^2 = -12(y + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \((x - 2)^2 = -12(y + 1)\).

To solve for the vertex and focus of the given equation \( x^2 = -16y \):

**Step 1: Identify the Standard Form**

The standard form of a vertical parabola opening upward or downward is:
\[ (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \]
where \((h, k)\) is the vertex and \( p \) determines the direction and width.

**Step 2: Compare with the Given Equation**

Given equation:
\[ x^2 = -16y \]

Comparing this to the standard form, we can see that it's already in a similar structure. Let's rewrite it for clarity:
\[ (x - 0)^2 = 4p(y - 0) \]
So,
\[ h = 0 \]
\[ k = 0 \]
And,
\[ 4p = -16 \]

**Step 3: Solve for \( p \)**

From the equation:
\[ 4p = -16 \]
Divide both sides by 4:
\[ p = -4 \]

**Step 4: Determine the Direction of the Parabola**

Since \( p \) is negative, the parabola opens downward.

**Step 5: Find the Focus**

The vertex form for a vertical parabola opening downward (since it's negative):
- Vertex at \( (h, k) = (0, 0) \)
- Focus is located at \( (h, k + p) \)

Substitute the known values:
\[ h = 0 \]
\[ k + p = 0 + (-4) = -4 \]

So, the focus is at \( (0, -4) \).

**Final Answer**

The vertex of the parabola is at \( (0, 0) \), and the focus is located at \( (0, -4) \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   Start by grouping the x terms and y terms together.
   \[
   X^2 + 6X + Y^2 -4Y = 12
   \]

   Take the coefficient of \( X \) (which is 6), divide by 2 to get 3, then square it to get 9.
   Add and subtract this value within the equation:
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X + 9) - 9
   \]

   Take the coefficient of \( Y \) (which is -4), divide by 2 to get -2, then square it to get 4.
   Add and subtract this value within the equation:
   \[
   (Y^2 - 4Y + 4) - 4
   \]
   
4. **Rewrite the Equation:**
   Incorporate the completed squares back into the equation:
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X + 9) + (Y^2 -4Y +4) = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   
5. **Simplify Both Sides:**
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   (X +3)^2 + (Y -2)^2 = 25
   \]

The given quadratic equation is \( x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y - 11 = 0 \). To convert it into the standard form of a circle's equation, we complete the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

1. Start by rearranging the equation:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. Complete the square for the \( x \) terms:
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 4 = (x - 2)^2
   \]
   
3. Complete the square for the \( y \) terms:
   \[
   y^2 + 2y + 1 = (y + 1)^2
   \]

4. Add these squares to both sides of the equation:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 11 + 4 + 1 = 16
   \]

The standard form is now \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \), representing a circle with center at \( (2, -1) \) and radius \( 4 \).

To find the radius of the circle with center (-2, 5) passing through (1, 9):

1. **Start with the standard equation of a circle:**
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]

2. **Plug in the point (1, 9):**
   \[
   (1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
3. **Simplify:**
   \[
   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]
   
4. **Find the radius \( r \):**
   \[
   r = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

To convert the given equation of an ellipse into its standard form, follow these steps:

1. **Start with the original equation:**
   \[
   9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36
   \]

2. **Divide both sides by 36** to normalize the equation:
   \[
   \frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
3. **Simplify each term:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]
   
Now, the equation is in its standard form:
\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1}
\]

This represents an ellipse with a semi-major axis of length \( b = 3 \) along the y-axis and a semi-minor axis of length \( a = 2 \) along the x-axis.

The midpoint formula is ((x1 + x2)/2, (y1 + y2)/2). Plugging in, we get ((40 + (-40))/2, (0+0)/2) = (0, 0). So the center is at the origin.

Given that the foci are on the x-axis, c is 40 because each focus is 40 units away from the center. So, c=40.

The length of the major axis is given as 100. I recall that the major axis length is 2a, so if 2a = 100, then a = 50. Therefore, a^2 would be 2500.

Now, for an ellipse, there's the relationship between a, b, and c: a^2 = b^2 + c^2. Wait, no, that can't be right because if I plug in c=40 and a=50, then 50^2 should equal b^2 + 40^2.

Let me write that down:

a^2 = b^2 + c^2

So,

50^2 = b^2 + 40^2

Calculating the squares:

2500 = b^2 + 1600

Subtracting 1600 from both sides:

b^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900

Therefore, b = √900 = 30.

So now I have a=50 and b=30. Plugging these back into the standard equation of the ellipse centered at (0,0) with major axis along x-axis:

(x^2/50^2) + (y^2/30^2) = 1

Which simplifies to:

x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

So that's the equation of the ellipse.
</think>

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a\) is the semi-major axis and \(b\) is the semi-minor axis. 

Given:
- Foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0): The center is at (0, 0).
- Length of major axis: 100 ⇒ \(2a = 100 \Rightarrow a = 50\).
- Distance from center to focus (\(c\)) = 40.

Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\) for an ellipse:
\(40^2 = 50^2 - b^2\)
⇒ \(1600 = 2500 - b^2\)
⇒ \(b^2 = 900 ⇒ b = 30\).

Thus, the equation is \(\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1\).

The problem involves finding the center of an ellipse with foci at \((\pm30, 0)\) and a major axis length of 100.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understanding the Given Information:**
   - The major axis of the ellipse is along the x-axis because the foci are on the x-axis.
   - The foci are located at \((\pm30, 0)\), so \(c = 30\).
   - The length of the major axis is 100, which means the semi-major axis (\(a\)) is half of this value: \(a = 50\).

2. **Determining the Center:**
   - The center of an ellipse lies at the midpoint between its two foci.
   - For foci at \((c, 0)\) and \((-c, 0)\), the midpoint is calculated as:
     \[
     \left( \frac{c + (-c)}{2}, \frac{0 + 0}{2} \right) = (0, 0)
     \]
   - Therefore, the center of the ellipse is at the origin.

**Final Answer:**
The center of the ellipse is located at \(\boxed{(0, 0)}\).

To determine the meaning of when \( x = \pm 1 \) on the given ellipse defined by \( \frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 = 9 \):

1. Substitute \( x = \pm 1 \) into the ellipse equation.
2. Calculate the corresponding y-coordinates:
   \[
   \frac{(\pm 1)^2}{4} + y^2 = 9
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   \frac{1}{4} + y^2 = 9
   \]
   \[
   y^2 = 9 - \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   \[
   y^2 = \frac{35}{4}
   \]
   Taking the square root of both sides, we find:
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}
   \]

Thus, when \( x = \pm 1 \) on the ellipse, the corresponding points are \( (\pm 1, \pm \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}) \).

### Final Answer
The meaning of when \( x = \pm 1 \) on the given ellipse is that these are the points where vertical lines intersecting the ellipse at those x-values. The corresponding y-coordinates can be calculated and result in the points \( (\pm 1, \pm \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2}) \). Therefore, the final answer is \boxed{(\pm 1, \pm \frac{\sqrt{35}}{2})}.

\[

First, I'll recall that the standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:

\[
\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where (h,k) is the center, and \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

Comparing this with our given equation:

- The center (h,k) is at (0,0).
- \( a^2 = 4 \), so \( a = 2 \).
- \( b^2 = 9 \), so \( b = 3 \).

This means the ellipse extends:
- Along the x-axis from -2 to +2.
- Along the y-axis from -3 to +3.

To visualize this, on a grid:
- The ellipse will stretch wider along the horizontal axis (since \( a > b \)).
- The major axis length is 2a = 4 units.
- The minor axis length is 2b = 6 units.

Therefore, all points (x,y) that satisfy the equation lie within this stretched oval shape centered at the origin.

To find the equation of the ellipse with the given parameters:

- **Center**: (-2, 1)
- **Semi-major axis (a)**: 8
- **Semi-minor axis (b)**: 4 (since minor axis length is 8)

The standard form of an ellipse's equation when oriented vertically (major axis along y) is:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
\]
Plugging in the values:
- \(h = -2\), \(k = 1\)
- \(a^2 = 64\)
- \(b^2 = 16\)

Thus, the equation becomes:
\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{16} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{64} = 1
\]

But wait, in standard grid problems where you move from a point to another using rights and downs (or ups), the number of moves is determined by the differences in coordinates. However, here we're dealing with negative numbers because one coordinate goes down.

Wait, so starting at (0, 0) and going to (4, -3). To get from x=0 to x=4, I need 4 right moves. But what about the y-coordinate? Starting at 0 and ending at -3 means I have to go down by 3 units. So that would require 3 down moves.

So in total, the number of moves is 4 rights + 3 downs = 7 moves. Now, since each path is a sequence of these moves, the problem reduces to finding how many unique sequences there are where we arrange 4 R's and 3 D's.

This sounds like a combinations problem because I'm choosing positions for either R or D in the total number of steps. The formula for combinations is C(n, k) = n! / (k!(n - k)!), where n is the total number of moves, and k is the number of one type of move.

Here, n is 7 (4 + 3) and k can be either 4 or 3. Let's choose k=4 for R's. So it would be C(7,4). Alternatively, C(7,3) should give the same result because choosing where to place the D's among 7 steps is also possible.

Calculating C(7,4): 

C(7,4) = 7! / (4! * (7-4)!) = 7! / (4! * 3!) 
= (7*6*5*4!)/(4! * 3!) 
The 4! cancels out top and bottom, leaving (7*6*5)/(3*2*1)
= 210/6
= 35.

So the number of paths is 35. But wait, I'm supposed to consider negative y-coordinates as well. Does that affect anything? Maybe not because moving down just means subtracting from y, but since we're only concerned with the path count regardless of direction beyond R and D, it's still a combination problem.

- The length of the transverse axis (2a) = 60
- The length of the conjugate axis (2b) = 8

- \( 2a = 60 \) ⇒ \( a = \frac{60}{2} = 30 \)
- \( 2b = 8 \) ⇒ \( b = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \)

Assuming it's a horizontal hyperbola (since typically, without additional information, we take the transverse axis as horizontal):
\[ \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

**Step 3: Plug in the values of a and b**

Substituting \( a = 30 \) and \( b = 4 \):
\[ \frac{x^2}{30^2} - \frac{y^2}{4^2} = 1 \]
Simplifying:
\[ \frac{x^2}{900} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \]

So, the equation of the hyperbola is:

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{900} - \dfrac{y^2}{16} = 1}

The equation \( 4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36 \) is converted into its standard form by dividing each term by 36. 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Start with the given equation:
   \[
   4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36
   \]
2. Divide every term by 36 to normalize the right-hand side to 1:
   \[
   \frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
3. Simplify each fraction:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
   \]
   
The standard form of the hyperbola is thus achieved.

To find the eccentricity (\( e \)) of the hyperbola given by the equation:

\[
\frac{y^2}{8} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Step 1: Identify the Standard Form and Parameters**

The standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin, opening upwards and downwards, is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Comparing this with the given equation:

- \( a^2 = 8 \) ⇒ \( a = \sqrt{8} \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = \sqrt{16} = 4 \)

**Step 2: Use the Eccentricity Formula**

The eccentricity (\( e \)) of a hyperbola is given by:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]

Substituting the known values:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{8}} = \sqrt{1 + 2} = \sqrt{3}
\]

Given the expression \( x^{(2/8)}/4 - y^{\text{something}}/\text{something}=1 \), we need to determine the foci, asymptotes, and latus rectum of the hyperbola.

First, we interpret the given expression. Assuming it represents a standard hyperbola equation with denominators 64 (from \( x^2 \) term) and 16 (since divided by 4), we proceed with the assumption that the hyperbola equation is:

\[ \frac{x^2}{64} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \]

For this standard form of a hyperbola, \( a^2 = 64 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \). Thus, we have:
- \( a = 8 \)
- \( b = 4 \)

Next, we calculate the foci. The distance to the foci \( c \) is given by:

\[ c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5} \]

The asymptotes for a hyperbola in this form are given by:

\[ y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{4}{8}x = \pm \frac{1}{2}x \]

Finally, the length of the latus rectum is calculated as:

\[ 2 \times \frac{b^2}{a} = 2 \times \frac{16}{8} = 4 \]

### Final Answer

The foci are located at \boxed{(\pm 4\sqrt{5}, 0)}, the asymptotes are given by \( y = \pm \dfrac{1}{2}x \), and the latus rectum length is \boxed{4}.

I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through the point (3, 4) and has its vertex at y = 2.

First, I'll use the vertex form of a parabola's equation:
y = a(x - h)^2 + k

Given the vertex is on y = 2, but since it's not specified which x-coordinate, I'll assume h can be any value. However, without knowing h, I need another point to determine both a and h.

Assuming the vertex is at (h, 2), I substitute this into the equation:
y = a(x - h)^2 + 2

Now, using the point (3, 4):
4 = a(3 - h)^2 + 2

Subtracting 2 from both sides gives:
a(3 - h)^2 = 2
So,
a = 2 / (3 - h)^2

Since there are infinitely many possible values for h, I can choose one to find a specific equation. For simplicity, let's set h = 0.

Then,
a = 2 / (3)^2 = 2/9

Substituting back into the vertex form:
y = (2/9)x^2 + 2
</think>

To find the equation of a parabola that passes through the point \((3, 4)\) with its vertex at \(y = 2\), we can use the vertex form of a parabola's equation:

\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]

where \((h, k)\) is the vertex. Since the vertex lies on \(y = 2\), we have \(k = 2\). Let's assume the x-coordinate of the vertex is \(h\).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Vertex Form:**
   
   \[
   y = a(x - h)^2 + 2
   \]

2. **Substitute the Point (3, 4):**
   
   The parabola passes through the point \((3, 4)\). Plugging these values into the equation:
   
   \[
   4 = a(3 - h)^2 + 2
   \]
   
3. **Solve for \(a\):**
   
   Subtract 2 from both sides:
   
   \[
   a(3 - h)^2 = 2 \\
   a = \frac{2}{(3 - h)^2}
   \]
   
4. **Determine the Value of \(h\):**
   
   Since no specific vertex x-coordinate is provided, we can choose any value for \(h\). For simplicity, let's assume \(h = 0\) (the vertex is at the origin vertically).
   
5. **Find \(a\) with \(h = 0\):**
   
   \[
   a = \frac{2}{(3 - 0)^2} = \frac{2}{9}
   \]
   
6. **Final Equation of the Parabola:**
   
   Substitute \(a = \frac{2}{9}\) and \(h = 0\) back into the vertex form:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{2}{9}x^2 + 2
   \]

**Answer:**

\boxed{\,y=\dfrac{2}{9}x^{2}+2\,}

The equation of the parabola is \boxed{y^2 = 4p(x - p + 1)} where \( p \) is a non-zero real number.

Given that the directrix of the parabola is fixed at \( x = -1 \) and the focus can vary along another line, we need to find the equation of the parabola.

For a horizontal parabola opening to the right, the standard form is:
\[ (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \]
where the vertex is at \( (h, k) \), the focus is at \( (h + p, k) \), and the directrix is \( x = h - p \).

Given the directrix \( x = -1 \), we have:
\[ h - p = -1 \implies h = p - 1 \]

Thus, the vertex of the parabola is at \( (p - 1, k) \). Since the problem does not specify any vertical shift, we assume \( k = 0 \).

The equation of the parabola then becomes:
\[ y^2 = 4p(x - (p - 1)) \]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{y^2 = 4p(x - p + 1)}
\]
where \( p \) is a non-zero real number.

To find the vertex of the parabola given by \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), I'll use the vertex formula. 

First, identify the coefficients:
- The coefficient of \( x^2 \) is 3.
- The coefficient of \( x \) is 6.

Next, calculate the x-coordinate of the vertex using the formula \( x = -\frac{b}{2a} \):
\[ x = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1 \]

Now, substitute this x-value back into the original equation to find the corresponding y-coordinate:
\[ Y(-1) = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2 \]

Therefore, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2).

The quadratic equation passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) is determined by solving for coefficients a, b, and c in the general form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). By substituting each point into this equation, we set up three equations:

1. From (0,4): \( 4 = c \)
2. From (-1,3): \( 3 = a - b + 4 \) leading to \( a - b = -1 \)
3. From (2,8): \( 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \) simplifying to \( 4a + 2b = 4 \)

Solving the system of equations gives \( a = \frac{1}{3} \), \( b = \frac{4}{3} \), and \( c = 4 \). Thus, the equation is:

\[

The standard form of a vertical parabola is \((x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)\), where \((h, k)\) is the vertex and \(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also to the directrix).

Given:
- Vertex: \((2, -1)\)
- Directrix: \(y = -4\)

Since the directrix is below the vertex, the parabola opens upward. The distance between the vertex and the directrix is:

\[ |k - (-p)| = |(-1) + p| = 3 \]

Solving for \(p\):

\[ |-1 + p| = 3 \]
Since it's opening upward, we have:
\[ -1 + p = 3 \]
\[ p = 4 \]

Now plug the values into the standard form:

\[ (x - 2)^2 = 4(4)(y + 1) \]
Simplify:

\[ (x - 2)^2 = 16(y + 1) \]

The equation of the parabola is:

To find the sum from \(i = 0\) to \(n\) of \(\binom{n}{i} (-1)^i A^{n-i}\), we can use the binomial theorem. 

First, we factor out \(A^n\) from each term:

\[
\sum_{i=0}^n \binom{n}{i} (-1)^i A^{n-i} = A^n \sum_{i=0}^n \binom{n}{i} \left( -\frac{1}{A} \right)^i
\]

Recognizing this as the binomial expansion of \((1 - 1/A)^n\), we have:

\[
A^n \left( 1 - \frac{1}{A} \right)^n = A^n \cdot \left( \frac{A - 1}{A} \right)^n = (A - 1)^n
\]

Thus, the sum is equal to \(\boxed{(A - 1)^n}\).

Alright, so I have this problem where I need to find the equation of a circle given the endpoints of its diameter. The endpoints provided are (-2, 1) and (6, -3). Okay, let me think about how to approach this step by step.

First off, I remember that the equation of a circle in standard form is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius. So, my goal is to find both the center and the radius.

The first step is to find the midpoint of the diameter because that will give me the center of the circle. The formula for the midpoint between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:

\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]

Plugging in the given points (-2, 1) and (6, -3):

For \(h\):
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]

For \(k\):
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]

So, the center of the circle is at (2, -1).

Next, I need to find the radius. The radius is half the length of the diameter. To find the length of the diameter, I'll use the distance formula between the two endpoints:

\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Substituting the points:

\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{(8)^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
\]

Simplifying \(\sqrt{80}\), I know that 80 is 16 times 5, so:

\[
d = \sqrt{16 \times 5} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]

Since the radius \(r\) is half of the diameter:

\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

Now, I can square the radius to get \(r^2\) for the equation:

\[
r^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

With both the center and the radius squared known, I can now write the equation of the circle. Plugging \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r^2 = 20\) into the standard form:

\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = 20
\]

Simplifying that, since subtracting a negative is adding:

\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

So, the equation of the circle with endpoints (-2, 1) and (6, -3) on its diameter is \((x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20\).

   - \( k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1 \)

   - Distance between endpoints:

     \]
   - Radius:
     \[
     r = \frac{d}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
     \]

- \( a \) is the semi-major axis length.
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.
- The distance from the center to each focus is \( c \), and it's related to \( a \) and \( b \) by the equation \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \).

Given that the major axis length is 100, I think that means the full length of the major axis is 100. So, the semi-major axis \( a \) would be half of that, which is 50.

Now, since the foci are at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), their distance from the center (which should be the origin because both foci are symmetric about (0,0)) is 40. So, \( c = 40 \).

Using the relationship between a, b, and c:

\[
c^2 = a^2 - b^2
\]

Plugging in the known values:

\[
40^2 = 50^2 - b^2
\]
\[
1600 = 2500 - b^2
\]
\[
b^2 = 2500 - 1600
\]
\[
b^2 = 900
\]

So, \( b = \sqrt{900} = 30 \).

Now that I have both a and b squared, I can plug them into the standard equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{30^2} = 1
\]
\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
\]

So, the equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1 \).
</think>

The equation of the ellipse with foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), and a major axis length of 100 is:

$$
\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{2500} + \dfrac{y^{2}}{900} = 1}
$$

   In an ellipse, the relationship between these parameters is given by:

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We need to find the equation of an ellipse given its center, length of the major axis, and length of the minor axis.

First, **understand the standard form of an ellipse**:
The general equation for an ellipse centered at \((h, k)\) is:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \(a\) and \(b\) are the lengths of the semi-major axis and semi-minor axis, respectively. We'll determine which one is larger to assign them correctly.

**Given:**
- Center \((-2, 1)\)
- Length of major axis (\(2a\)) = 10
- Length of minor axis (\(2b\)) = 8

**Step 1:** Calculate \(a\) and \(b\).
- Semi-major axis length (\(a\)):
  
  \[
  a = \frac{2a}{2} = \frac{10}{2} = 5
  \]
  
- Semi-minor axis length (\(b\)):
  
  \[
  b = \frac{2b}{2} = \frac{8}{2} = 4
  \]

**Step 2:** Plug the values into the standard equation.
We know that:
- \(h = -2\)
- \(k = 1\)
- \(a^2 = 5^2 = 25\)
- \(b^2 = 4^2 = 16\)

Now, substitute these into the general form:

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{25} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x + 2)^2}{25} + \dfrac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1}
\]

The hyperbola is given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{8} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Hyperbola Form:**
   The given equation is in the standard form of a horizontally opening hyperbola:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   Here, \( a^2 = 8 \) and \( b^2 = 4 \).

2. **Determine Parameters:**
   - Calculate \( a \):
     \[
     a = \sqrt{a^2} = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2}
     \]
   - Calculate \( b \):
     \[
     b = \sqrt{b^2} = \sqrt{4} = 2
     \]

3. **Find the Foci:**
   The distance from the center to each focus is \( c \), where:
   \[
   c^2 = a^2 + b^2 = 8 + 4 = 12 \implies c = \sqrt{12} = 2\sqrt{3}
   \]
   Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, the foci are at \( (c, 0) \) and \( (-c, 0) \):
   \[
   (\pm 2\sqrt{3}, 0)
   \]

4. **Calculate the Latus Rectum:**
   The length of the latus rectum is given by:
   \[
   \text{Length} = \frac{2b^2}{a} = \frac{2(4)}{2\sqrt{2}} = \frac{8}{2\sqrt{2}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{2}} = 2\sqrt{2}
   \]

5. **Determine the Asymptotes:**
   For a horizontal hyperbola, the asymptotes are:
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{2}{2\sqrt{2}}x = \pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x = \pm \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}x
   \]

\boxed{a \neq 0} \text{ and } \boxed{b \neq 0}

The given problem involves determining the equation of a vertical hyperbola with vertices at \((0, \pm 5)\) and foci at \((0, \pm 3)\). However, based on the properties of hyperbolas, this configuration is impossible because for any hyperbola, the distance from the center to the foci (\(c\)) must always be greater than the distance from the center to the vertices (\(a\)). In this case, \(a = 5\) and \(c = 3\), which violates the condition \(c > a\). Therefore, such a hyperbola cannot exist.

$\boxed{\text{No such hyperbola exists}}$
